The symplectic structure of the PGL n ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}) –Hitchin component Thanks: This work was partially supported by the National Science Foundation of the United States (grant DMS-2005656), and by the Scientific and Technological Research Council of Türkiye, TÜBİTAK (BİDEB-2221-(1059B211402137) and BİDEB-2214/A)
Francis Bonahon, Yaşar Sözen, Hatice Zeybek

Abstract
. The PGL n ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}) –Hitchin component Hit n ​ ( S ) \mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S) of a closed oriented surface is a preferred component of the character variety consisting of homomorphisms from the fundamental group of the surface to the projective linear group PGL n ​ ( ℝ ) \mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R}) . It admits a symplectic structure, defined by the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman symplectic form. The main result of the article is an explicit computation of this symplectic form in terms of certain global coordinates for Hit n ​ ( S ) \mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S) . A remarkable feature of this expression is that its coefficients are constant.



Introduction

For an oriented closed surface
S
of negative Euler characteristic, the
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
–Hitchin component

\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
is a preferred component of the character variety

\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})}(S)=\{\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\}/\kern-4.0pt/\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})

consisting of group homomorphisms
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
from the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(S)
of the surface to the projective linear group
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, considered modulo the action of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
by conjugation.

Fundamental results of Hitchin
[
Hit92
]
, Labourie
[
Lab06
]
and Fock-Goncharov
[
FG06
]
, as well as subsequent work by many researchers triggered by these breakthroughs, have showed that the
Hitchin representations
, namely the homomorphisms
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
representing elements of
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
, have very strong geometric properties. The general theme is that these properties are very similar to the ones observed in the case
n=2
where, for the identification between
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and the isometry group of the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, Hitchin representations correspond to the monodromies of hyperbolic metrics on the surface
S
. For instance, Hitchin representations are injective with discrete image, the action of the mapping class group on
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
is discontinuous, and the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
is smooth and diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{-\chi(S)(n^{2}-1)}
, where
\chi(S)<0
is the Euler characteristic of the surface.

The Hitchin diffeomorphism between
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
and
\mathbb{R}^{-\chi(S)(n^{2}-1)}
was based on the theory of Higgs bundles. A different parametrization, combining the dynamical properties uncovered in
[
Lab06
]
with the representation theoretic arguments of
[
FG06
]
, was introduced in
[
BD17
]
.
The Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
and, more generally, the smooth part of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})}(S)
, comes with a preferred symplectic structure, the
Atiyah-Bott-Goldman symplectic form

[
AB83
,
Gol84
]
. The main result of the current article is an explicit computation of this symplectic form in terms of the global coordinates for
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
developed in
[
BD17
]
.

The coordinates of
[
BD17
]
were inspired by earlier work of Fock and Goncharov
[
FG06
]
for surfaces with at least one puncture. For such a punctured surface
S
, Fock and Goncharov developed a theory of positive framed representations
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
that is parallel to that of Hitchin representations. In particular, they proved that the space
\mathrm{P}_{n}(S)
of positive framed characters is also diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{-\chi(S)(n^{2}-1)}
, by constructing explicit global coordinates for this space, associated to an ideal triangulation of the surface
S
. Because of the punctures, the space
\mathrm{P}_{n}(S)
does not admit a symplectic structure, but it is endowed with a Poisson structure and Fock and Goncharov explicitly compute this Poisson structure in their coordinates.

Figure 1.
The dots indexing the Fock-Goncharov coordinates, for
n=6
.

Before describing the generalized Fock-Goncharov coordinates of
[
BD17
]
it may be useful, for perspective, to remind the reader of the original Fock-Goncharov coordinates of
[
FG06
]
for punctured surfaces. Incidentally, we are here considering the so-called
X
–coordinates for positive framed
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
–characters, as opposed to the
A
–coordinates of the dual
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
–based theory; see
[
FG06
]
. Let
S
be a punctured surface of negative Euler characteristic, obtained by removing a nonempty set of punctures
\{p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{k}\}
from a closed oriented surface
\bar{S}
. The Fock-Goncharov coordinates for the space
\mathrm{P}_{n}(S)
of positive framed characters use an
ideal triangulation
of the surface
S
, namely a triangulation of the closed surface
\bar{S}
whose vertices are exactly the punctures
p_{i}
. The Fock-Goncharov coordinates are then indexed by a certain number of dots drawn on the surface. More precisely, there are
n-1
hollow (white) dots on each edge of the ideal triangulation, and the interior of each face
T
is endowed with
\frac{1}{2}(n-1)(n-2)
solid (black) dots arranged as an embedded copy of the
discrete triangle

\Theta_{n}=\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3};a,b,c\geqslant 1\text{ and }a+b+c=n\},

in such a way that the vertices of the discrete triangle point towards the corners of the triangle
T
. See Figure
1
. An elementary Euler characteristic argument shows that there is a total of
-\chi(S)(n^{2}-1)
such solid and hollow dots, a number that is also the dimension of the space
\mathrm{P}_{n}(S)
of positive framed characters.

Given a positive framed character
[\rho]\in\mathrm{P}_{n}(S)
, Fock and Goncharov associate a
triangle invariant

\tau_{\delta}(\rho)\in\mathbb{R}
to each solid dot
\delta
in a face of the triangulation, and a
shearing invariant

\sigma_{\delta}(\rho)\in\mathbb{R}
to each hollow dot
\delta
in an edge. To be completely accurate, the original Fock-Goncharov
X
–coordinates are positive numbers, and we are taking here their logarithms. Fock and Goncharov then show that these invariants provide a diffeomorphism from the space
\mathrm{P}_{n}(S)
of positive framed characters to
\mathbb{R}^{-\chi(S)(n^{2}-1)}
.

Figure 2.
A train track.

A closed surface has no ideal triangulation, since there is no puncture where to put the vertices. The parametrization developed in
[
BD17
]
replaces the ideal triangulation by the data of a maximal geodesic lamination
\lambda
and of a train track carrying
\lambda
; see §
1.2
for precise definitions. A geodesic lamination usually has a very intricate dynamical structure, but a train track is a very combinatorial object. More precisely, a
train track

\Psi
in the surface
S
is a trivalent graph embedded in
S
, whose edges are smoothly embedded and such that, at each vertex, the three adjacent edges are tangent to a single line with two edges going in one direction and the remaining edge in the other direction. See Figure
2
for an example. This property implies that the completion of the complement
S-\Psi
has sharp corners occurring at the vertices of
\Psi
and, because the geodesic lamination is maximal, there is the additional property that each component of
S-\Psi
is a triangle, namely a disk with 3 corners in its boundary.

In accordance with the american railroad terminology, the vertices of the train track
\Psi
are called its
switches
, and its edges are its
branches
.

As in the original framework of
[
FG06
]
, the generalized Fock-Goncharov coordinates of
[
BD17
]
are associated to dots drawn on the surface, except that there are many more of them. More precisely, there are
n-1
hollow dots on each branch of the train track
\Psi
, and
\frac{1}{2}(n-1)(n-2)
solid dots in each triangle component
U
of the complement
S-\Psi
, arranged as a copy of the discrete triangle
\Theta_{n}
in such a way that the vertices of
\Theta_{n}
point toward the corners of
U
. In particular, there is a total of
-\chi(S)(n-1)(n+7)
such dots. See Figure
3
for an illustration. A Hitchin character
[\rho]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
then determines a
triangle invariant

\tau_{\delta}(\rho)\in\mathbb{R}
associated to each solid dot
\delta
in the complement
S-\Psi
, and a
shearing invariant

\sigma_{\delta}(\rho)\in\mathbb{R}
associated to each hollow dot on a branch of
\Psi
. We should probably emphasize that these invariants depend, not just on the train track
\Psi
, but also on the maximal geodesic lamination
\lambda
carried by
\Psi
.

Figure 3.
The dots indexing the generalized Fock-Goncharov coordinates, for
n=6
.

The total number
-\chi(S)(n-1)(n+7)
of dots is significantly more than the dimension
-\chi(S)(n^{2}-1)
of
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
. Indeed, whereas the original Fock-Goncharov coordinates were independent, the generalized Fock-Goncharov coordinates satisfy a certain number of linear relations associated to the switches of the train track
\Psi
. More precisely, by our definition of train tracks, a switch
s
of
\Psi
has two incoming branches
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
and a single outgoing branch
e_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}
, with
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
to the left of
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
when oriented toward
s
. We orient
e_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}
away from
s
. The switch also determines a component
U_{s}
of
S-\Psi
, located between
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
near
s
. Then, for
a=1
,
2
, …,
n-1
, the
a
–th switch relation
associated to
s
states that

\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}(a)}(\rho)=\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(a)}(\rho)+\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(a)}(\rho)-\sum_{\delta}\tau_{\delta}(\rho)

where
\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}(a)
,
\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(a)
and
\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(a)
are the
a
–th dots in the branches
e_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}
,
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
for the orientations specified above, and where the sum is over the
n-a-1
solid dots
\delta
in the component
U_{s}
of
S-\Psi
that, in the corresponding discrete triangle, are in the
a
–th row parallel to the side of this discrete triangle that is opposite the vertex facing
s
. See Figure
4
for a graphical illustration of this switch relation, and §
1.3
for a more formal description.

\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}(a)

\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(a)

\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(a)

\delta

\delta

\delta

\delta

Figure 4.
The
a
–th switch relation for
n=8
and
a=3

The switch relations constrain the generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants
\tau_{\delta}(\rho)
and
\sigma_{\delta}(\rho)
to the linear subspace
L
of
\mathbb{R}^{-(n-1)(n+7)\chi(S)}
defined by these relations. In addition, these invariants must satisfy a certain
positivity condition
defined by finitely many strict linear inequalities, involving the transverse measures for the geodesic lamination
\lambda
and consequently based on the dynamical structure of the lamination.

The main result of
[
BD17
]
is that the map assigning its generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants to a Hitchin character
[\rho]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
defines a diffeomorphism from
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
to the polytope in
\mathbb{R}^{-(n-1)(n+7)\chi(S)}
defined by the switch relations and the positivity condition.

Going back to the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman symplectic form
\omega
, each dot
\delta
defines a function
\tau_{\delta}\colon[\rho]\mapsto\tau_{\delta}(\rho)
or
\sigma_{\delta}\colon[\rho]\mapsto\sigma_{\delta}(\rho)
on the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
. Our main result provides an explicit computation of
\omega
in terms of the differentials
d\tau_{\delta}
and
d\sigma_{\delta}
of these coordinate maps.

Theorem 1
.

The Atiyah-Bott-Goldman symplectic form
\omega
of the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
can be expressed in terms of the generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants as

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{U\text{ component of }S-\Psi}\ \sum_{\delta,\,\delta^{\prime}\in U}\tfrac{1}{2}C(\delta,\delta^{\prime})\,d\tau_{\delta}\wedge d\tau_{\delta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s\text{ switch of }\Psi}\ \sum_{\delta\in e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}\!,\,\delta^{\prime}\in e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}}C(\delta,\delta^{\prime})\,d\sigma_{\delta}\wedge d\sigma_{\delta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{s\text{ switch of }\Psi}\ \sum_{\delta\in e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}\!,\,\delta^{\prime}\in U_{s}}C(\delta,\delta^{\prime})\,d\sigma_{\delta}\wedge d\tau_{\delta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s\text{ switch of }\Psi}\ \sum_{\delta\in e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}\!,\,\delta^{\prime}\in U_{s}}C(\delta,\delta^{\prime})\,d\sigma_{\delta}\wedge d\tau_{\delta^{\prime}}

where the coefficients
C(\delta,\delta^{\prime})
are explicit integers associated to the dots
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
. In particular, these coefficients are constant.

More precisely,

C(\delta,\delta^{\prime})=\begin{cases}a^{\prime}b-ab^{\prime}&\text{if }a\leqslant a^{\prime},b\leqslant b^{\prime}\text{ and }c\geqslant c^{\prime}\\
ac^{\prime}-a^{\prime}c&\text{if }a\leqslant a^{\prime},b\geqslant b^{\prime}\text{ and }c\leqslant c^{\prime}\\
b^{\prime}c-bc^{\prime}&\text{if }a\leqslant a^{\prime},b\geqslant b^{\prime}\text{ and }c\geqslant c^{\prime}\\
b^{\prime}c-bc^{\prime}&\text{if }a\geqslant a^{\prime},b\leqslant b^{\prime}\text{ and }c\leqslant c^{\prime}\\
ac^{\prime}-a^{\prime}c&\text{if }a\geqslant a^{\prime},b\leqslant b^{\prime}\text{ and }c\geqslant c^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}b-ab^{\prime}&\text{if }a\geqslant a^{\prime},b\geqslant b^{\prime}\text{ and }c\leqslant c^{\prime}\end{cases}

if the solid dots
\delta
,
\delta^{\prime}\in U
are respectively associated to the points
(a,b,c)
,
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
of the discrete triangle
\Theta_{n}
, and

C(\delta,\delta^{\prime})=\begin{cases}a^{\prime}(n-a)&\text{if }a\geqslant a^{\prime}\\
a(n-a^{\prime})&\text{if }a\leqslant a^{\prime},\end{cases}

if:
\delta
is the
a
–th hollow dot in
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
\delta^{\prime}
is the
a^{\prime}
–th hollow dot in
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
; or
\delta
is the
a
–th hollow dot in
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
\delta^{\prime}\in U_{s}
is a solid dot which, in the discrete triangle of
U_{s}
, is in the
a^{\prime}
–th row parallel to the side that is opposite
s
; or
\delta
is the
a
–th hollow dot in
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
and
\delta^{\prime}\in U_{s}
is a solid dot which, in the discrete triangle of
U_{s}
, is in the
a^{\prime}
–th row parallel to the side opposite
s
.

As an illustration, the formula is relatively simple when
n=3
. In this case, let
\delta_{s}
be the unique solid
dot contained in the component
U_{s}
of
S-\Psi
associated to the switch
s
. Also, let
\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(a)
and
\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(a)
be the
a
–th dots in the incoming branches
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
of
s
, as in our statement of the Switch Condition and in Figure
4
.

Corollary 2
.

When
n=3
, the symplectic form of the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(S)
is equal to

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\text{ switch of }\Psi}2\left(d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(1)}\wedge d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(1)}+d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(2)}\wedge d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s\text{ switch of }\Psi}\left(d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(1)}\wedge d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(2)}+d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(2)}\wedge d\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s\text{ switch of }\Psi}\left(2\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(1)}+\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}(2)}-2\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(1)}-\sigma_{\delta_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}(2)}\right)\wedge d\tau_{\delta_{s}}.\qed

Remark 3
.

We have chosen to restrict attention to trivalent train tracks for the sake of the exposition. The reader familiar with this concept should have no problem extending Theorem
1
(and the definition of the switch relations) to general train tracks carrying the maximal geodesic lamination
\lambda
, where some switches are allowed to be adjacent to more than three branches. This would result in a significant increase in notational complexity for the statements, but tends to be more convenient for explicit computations.

Remark 4
.

The article
[
BD14
]
, intended as an easier version of
[
BD17
]
for the case when the geodesic lamination
\lambda
has finitely many leaves, develops a set of coordinates for the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
that looks very much like that of
[
BD17
]
. In fact, these two sets of coordinates coincide for the most part, but they differ very substantially in the way they measure the shear along a closed leaves of
\lambda
. The outcome is that, as already observed in
[
SZ17
]
, the coordinates of
[
BD14
]
are not well adapted to the symplectic form
\omega
. Indeed, the expression of
\omega
in these coordinates involves coefficients that are not constant and depend on the point of
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
considered.

Theorem
1
is proved as Theorem
49
in §
7
, using a notation scheme that is more formula-oriented and less graphical than the one used in this Introduction (see §
1.3
for the correspondence).
The Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form is defined
[
Gol84
]
as a cup-product, using the Weil identification of the tangent space
T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
with the cohomology space
H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
. The main technical part of the article, requiring subtle analytic estimates to guarantee convergence, is concentrated in §§
4
-
5
and is devoted to the explicit construction of a simplicial cocycle
c_{V}\in C^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
representing the cohomology class associated to a tangent vector
V\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
. This part involves a reconstruction of a Hitchin representation from its generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants, which is more explicit than the one used in
[
BD17
]
and may be of independent interest. After this, relatively straightforward combinatorial and algebraic computations in §
6
provide an estimate for the value of the cup-product. In a final step, the estimate is shown to be invariant under the operation of zipper-opening for train tracks described in §
5.4
, and is therefore exactly equal to the value of the cup-product.

Theorem
1
is the natural extension of the results of
[
SB01
]
for the case where
n=2
, and of
[
Zey21
]
for the restriction of
\omega
to the slices of
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
where the triangle invariants
\tau_{\rho}(d)
are constant. A version of Theorem
1
was obtained by Zhe Sun and Tengren Zhang
[
SZ17
]
for the case where the geodesic lamination is restricted to have only finitely many leaves and where the convergence arguments are easier. We actually rely on several tools developed by these authors, in particular the eruptions introduced in
[
SWZ20
]
(see §
3.3
), the algebraic computation of Lemma
42
, and the barriers of §
5.1
which enabled us to simplify an earlier approach of ours.

1.
The
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
–Hitchin component

1.1.
Hitchin characters and Hitchin representations

For a closed surface
S
of negative Euler characteristic, the
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
–Hitchin component

\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
is a preferred component of the character variety

\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})}(S)=\{\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\}/\kern-4.0pt/\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})

consisting of group homomorphisms
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
from the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(S)
of the surface to the projective linear group
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, considered modulo the action of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
by conjugation. More precisely, the monodromy
\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
of a hyperbolic metric on
S
can be extended to a homomorphism
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
by composing it with the unique
n
–dimensional irreducible representation
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. The Hitchin component is the component of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})}(S)
that contains the characters thus associated to hyperbolic metrics.

We will refer to elements of
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
as
Hitchin characters
, while homomorphisms
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
representing a Hitchin character
[\rho]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
will be
Hitchin representations
.
As indicated in the Introduction, Hitchin representations have very strong geometric properties.
We will particularly rely on two such features.

One is a dynamical property of Labourie
[
Lab06
]
which provides, for each Hitchin representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, a continuous
\rho
–equivariant
flag map

\mathcal{F}_{\rho}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
from the boundary at infinity
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
of the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
to the space
\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of flags in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This flag map is actually the unique continuous
\rho
–equivariant map
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The second property, due to Fock-Goncharov
[
FG06
]
, is more Lie theoretic and asserts that the flag map
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}
sends any finite family of distinct points
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
, …,
x_{k}\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
, occurring in this order in the circle
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
, to a family of flags
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x_{1})
,
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x_{2})
, …,
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x_{k})\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
that is positive, in a sense that we will discuss in a few more details in §
3.1
.

1.2.
Geodesic laminations and train tracks

The parametrization of
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
developed in
[
BD17
]
uses maximal geodesic laminations as a substitute for the ideal triangulations used by Fock and Goncharov
[
FG06
]
for punctured surfaces, since closed surfaces admit no ideal triangulation. The next section §
1.3
will give a brief description of the coordinates of
[
BD17
]
, but we first give some background on geodesic laminations. We refer to, for instance,
[
Thu23
,
CEG87
,
CB88
,
PH92
]
for details about these facts.

Since we are restricting attention to surfaces of negative Euler characteristic, we can endow the closed surface
S
with an arbitrary riemannian metric
m_{0}
of negative curvature. A
geodesic lamination
(or
m_{0}
–geodesic lamination
if we want to emphasize the dependence on the auxiliary metric
m_{0}
) is a closed subset
\lambda\subset S
that can be decomposed as the union of a family of disjoint simple geodesics, where a simple geodesic is a complete
m_{0}
–geodesic that does not intersect itself (but may be closed). These geodesics are the
leaves
of the geodesic lamination.

A simple example is provided by a family of finitely many disjoint simple closed geodesics. This example can be expanded by adding a few infinite geodesics that spiral around the closed geodesics. However, a typical geodesic lamination is usually much more complicated, and can locally consist of a Cantor set of disjoint geodesics. This is often the case with those geodesic laminations that arise from a geometric context, such as the stable and unstable measured geodesic laminations of a pseudo-Anosov diffeomorphism, or the bending lamination of a kleinian group.

Although the definition given here depends on the choice of a negatively curved metric
m_{0}
on the surface
S
, geodesic laminations can actually be described in an intrinsic way that make them independent of this choice, for instance in terms of the boundary at infinity
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
of the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
. As such, geodesic lamination intrinsically are topological objects associated to the surface.

A geodesic lamination is
maximal
if it is maximal for inclusion among all geodesic laminations.

Proposition 5
.

(1)

Every geodesic lamination is contained in a maximal geodesic lamination.

(2)

A geodesic lamination
\lambda
is maximal if and only if its complement
S-\lambda
consists of finitely many ideal triangles, each bounded by three infinite geodesics in
\lambda
. In addition, the number of these triangles is equal to
-2\chi(S)
, where
\chi(S)
is the Euler characteristic of
S
. ∎

In practice, exactly as a Cantor set looks like a finite set at finite resolution, a geodesic lamination drawn on a surface looks like a train track as defined in the Introduction. This can be formalized as follows.

A (trivalent)
train track neighborhood
for the
m_{0}
–geodesic lamination
\lambda
is a closed neighborhood
\Phi
of
\lambda
which can be decomposed as a union of finitely many embedded rectangles
R_{i}
, called its
branches
, such that

(1)

each rectangle
R_{i}\cong[0,1]\times[0,1]
is foliated by arcs
\{x\}\times[0,1]
, called its
ties
, and the leaves of
\lambda
are transverse to these ties;

(2)

the points of a branch
R_{i}\cong[0,1]\times[0,1]
that belong to another branch
R_{j}
are exactly the points of the boundary ties
\{0,1\}\times[0,1]
;

(3)

the points of
S
locally belonging to at least two branches form a family of disjoint arcs, called the
switch ties
of the train track neighborhood;

(4)

each switch tie is locally adjacent to one branch
R_{i}
on one side, and to exactly two branches
R_{j}
,
R_{k}
on the other side.

It follows from this general description that the closure of the complement
S-\Phi
has corners, corresponding to points where three distinct branches meet. We add one more global condition.

(5)

no component of
S-\Phi
is a disk with 0, 1 or 2 corners in its boundary.

\lambda

\lambda

\lambda

a switch tie

another tie

R_{i}

R_{j}

R_{k}

Figure 5.
The local model of a train track neighborhood near a switch tie

A fundamental property is that every geodesic lamination
\lambda
admits arbitrarily small train track neighborhoods.

Proposition 6
.

Let
U
be a component of the complement
S-\Phi
of a train track neighborhood
\Phi
of the maximal geodesic lamination
\lambda
, and let
T
be the component of
S-\lambda
that contains it. Then, the foliation of
\Phi\cap T
induced by the ties of
\Phi
is of the type illustrated in Figure
6
. In particular,
U
is a triangle, namely a disk with three corners in its boundary, and each corner of
U
points toward a unique vertex of the ideal triangle
T
.

This establishes a one-to-one correspondence between the components
U
of
S-\Phi
and the components
T
of
S-\lambda
, as well as a one-to-one correspondence between the corners of the boundary of
U
and the vertices of the ideal triangle
T
.
∎

T

Figure 6.
The train track neighborhood
\Phi
and a component
T
of
S-\lambda

From a train track neighborhood
\Phi
of the
m_{0}
–geodesic lamination
\lambda
, one easily obtains a train track
\Psi
in the sense of the Introduction, by collapsing each tie of
\Phi
to a single point. In this situation, we will say that
\lambda
is
(strongly) carried
by the train track
\Psi
.

We will often use the same letter to denote a branch
e
of the train track neighborhood
\Phi
and the corresponding branch
e
of the train track
\Psi
associated to
\Phi
, or for the switch tie
s
of
\Phi
and the corresponding switch
s
of
\Psi
.

Remark 7
.

The above definitions heavily depend on the auxiliary negatively curved metric
m_{0}
chosen to describe geodesic laminations. There is a more topological point of view, independent of the choice of the metric
m_{0}
, in which a geodesic lamination is
weakly carried
by the train track
\Psi
if, for every leaf
g
of
\lambda
, there is a train route in
\Psi
that is homotopic to
g
by a homotopy moving points by a uniformly bounded amount (the Fellow Traveller Property). All the results of the current article (as well as those of
[
BD17
]
) could be rephrased in this more topological framework, but at the expense of increased complexity in the exposition. We have chosen to restrict attention to the more restricted (metric dependent) viewpoint, but experts should have no problem extending our arguments to the more general setup.

1.3.
Generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants

Let
\lambda
be a maximal geodesic lamination in the surface
S
that is carried by a trivalent train track
\Psi
, in the sense of §
1.2
. By Proposition
6
, each component of
S-\Psi
is a triangle, namely a disk with three boundary corners (the vertices of the triangle) located at switches of
\Psi
.

The parametrization of
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
associated to
\lambda
and
\Psi
that is developed in
[
BD17
]
is based on two distinct types of invariants for a Hitchin representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

The first type are the
triangle invariants

\tau^{abc}_{\rho}(U,s)\in\mathbb{R}
. These are indexed by the components
U
of the complement
S-\Psi
, the vertices
s
of the triangle
U
, and the points
(a,b,c)
in the
discrete triangle

\Theta_{n}=\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3};a,b,c\geqslant 1\text{ and }a+b+c=n\}.

These triangle invariants satisfy the
Rotation Condition
that, if
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
,
s_{3}
are the three vertices of the triangle
U
, indexed counterclockwise in this order around
U
,

\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(U,s_{1})=\tau_{\rho}^{bca}(U,s_{2})=\tau_{\rho}^{cab}(U,s_{3}).

The second type of invariant consists of the
shearing invariants

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)\in\mathbb{R}
, indexed by the oriented branches
e
of the train track
\Psi
and by the points
(a,b)
in the
discrete interval

\mathrm{I}_{n}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2};a,b\geqslant 1\text{ and }a+b=n\}.

These are constrained by the
Orientation Reversal Relation
that, if
\overline{e}
is obtained by reversing the orientation of the branch
e
,

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\overline{e})=\sigma_{\rho}^{ba}(e).

There are two additional constraints for the invariants
\tau^{abc}_{\rho}(U,s)
and
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
. The main one consists of the
Switch Relations
for the shearing invariants
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}
, defined as follows. By our assumption that the train track
\Psi
is trivalent, there are three branches
e_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}
,
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
,
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
of
\Psi
that are adjacent to each switch
s
, with
e_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}
on one side of
s
while
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
are on the other side. We orient
e_{s}^{\mathrm{out}}
away from
s
and
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
and
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
toward
s
, with
e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}}
coming in on the left of
e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}}
(hence the names). See Figure
4
. Finally, let
U_{s}
be the triangle component of
S-\Psi
that has
s
as a vertex. The Switch Condition then asserts that

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e_{s}^{\mathrm{out}})=\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e_{s}^{\mathrm{left}})+\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e_{s}^{\mathrm{right}})-\sum_{b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=n-a}\tau_{\rho}^{ab^{\prime}c^{\prime}}(U_{s},s)

where the sum is over all
b^{\prime}
,
c^{\prime}
such that
(a,b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
is in the discrete triangle
\Theta_{n}
.

There is an additional
Positivity Condition
satisfied by the invariants, which we alluded to in the Introduction. However, it is here irrelevant as it is an open condition and we are only interested in local properties.

Remark 8
.

The shearing invariant
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
is called
\sigma^{\rho}_{a}(e)
in
[
BD17
]
, with no loss of information since
b=n-a
.

The reader should have no difficulty connecting these invariants
\tau^{abc}_{\rho}(U,s)
and
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
to the invariants
\tau_{\rho}(\delta)
and
\sigma_{\delta}(\rho)
of the Introduction. Namely, if we embed the discrete triangle
\Theta_{n}
in the component
U
of
S-\Psi
in such a way that the vertices
(n-2,1,1)
,
(1,n-2,1)
,
(1,1,n-2)
of
\Theta_{n}
respectively point toward corners
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
,
s_{3}
of
U
occurring counterclockwise in this order around
U
, then
\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(U,s_{1})
is equal to
\tau_{\delta}(\rho)
for the solid dot
\delta\in\Theta_{n}\subset U
corresponding to
(a,b,c)
. Similarly, if
e
is an oriented branch of the train track
\Psi
and if
\delta
is the hollow dot drawn on
e
that occurs in
a
–th position for the orientation of
e
, the shearing invariant
\sigma_{\delta}(\rho)
is equal to
\sigma_{\rho}^{a(n-a)}(e)
. The Rotation Condition and the Orientation Reversal Condition above guarantee the consistency of these identifications. Then, the Switch Relations of this section are easily seen to be equivalent to those described in the Introduction.

2.
The symplectic structure of the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)

2.1.
The Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form

The starting point in the definition of the Atiyah-Bott-Goldman form is Weil’s cohomological interpretation of tangent vectors to character varieties, pioneered in
[
Wei60
]
. In our context, this correspondence is the following.

Let
V\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
be a tangent vector of the Hitchin component at the point
[\rho]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
. Realize it as the tangent vector

V=\frac{d}{dt}[\rho_{t}]_{|t=0}

for a curve
t\mapsto[\rho_{t}]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
with
[\rho_{0}]=[\rho]
, namely for a family of group homomorphisms
\rho_{t}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
depending smoothly on a real parameter
t
and such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
.

For every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
, the derivative

c_{V}(\gamma)=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}

is an element of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{pgl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
. We will identify
\mathfrak{pgl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
to the more familiar Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
of
\mathrm{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, where computations will also be more explicit.

It can then be shown that the map
c_{V}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
so defined is a group cocycle twisted by the adjoint representation
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{GL}\left(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\right)
, in the sense that

c_{V}(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})=c_{V}(\gamma)+\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\gamma)\left(c_{V}(\gamma^{\prime})\right)=c_{V}(\gamma)+\rho(\gamma)c_{V}(\gamma^{\prime})\rho(\gamma)^{-1}.

This cocycle
c_{V}
defines a group cohomology class
[c_{V}]\in H^{1}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
twisted by the adjoint representation. Weil shows (in a more general setup) that this cohomology class
[c_{V}]
depends only on the tangent vector
V
, and that this establishes a one-to-one correspondence between the tangent space
T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
and the cohomology space
H^{1}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
.

Since the universal cover of
S
is contractible, the cohomology spaces
H^{1}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
and
H^{1}\left(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
are naturally isomorphic.

Now, two tangent vectors
V_{1}
,
V_{2}\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
give two cohomology classes
[c_{V_{1}}]
,
[c_{V_{2}}]\in H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
and, inspired by an earlier construction of Atiyah-Bott
[
AB83
]
for compact groups, Goldman
[
Gol84
]
considers their cup-product

[c_{V_{1}}]{\smallsmile}[c_{V_{2}}]\in H^{2}\left(S;\left(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\right)_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right).

The Killing form
K\colon\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is invariant under the adjoint representation, and therefore defines a homomorphism
K^{*}\colon H^{2}\left(S;\left(\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\right)_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)\to H^{2}\left(S;\mathbb{R}\right)
valued in the real untwisted cohomology. We can then evaluate the corresponding cohomology class on the fundamental class
[S]\in H_{2}(S;\mathbb{R})
, and define

\omega_{\rho}(V_{1},V_{2})=\left\langle K^{*}\left([c_{V_{1}}]{\smallsmile}[c_{V_{2}}]\right),[S]\right\rangle.

This construction provides an antisymmetric bilinear form
\omega_{\rho}
on each tangent space
T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
, which depends differentiably on the point
[\rho]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
. In other words, this defines a differential form
\omega
of degree 2 on the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
.

It follows from Poincaré duality that this differential form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}\big(\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)\big)
is non-degenerate at every point
[\rho]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
, and Goldman shows that it is also closed. As a consequence,
\omega
is a symplectic form on
\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
.

2.2.
A simplicial description of the Weil cohomology class

The group cohomology description of the Weil class
[c_{V}]\in H^{1}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)=H^{1}\left(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
associated to a tangent vector
V\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
is not very convenient for our purposes. In particular, to compute the cup-product of two such classes, we want a description of these classes in terms of the simplicial cohomology associated to a triangulation of the surface. We now describe a scheme that provides such a description, and will implement this scheme in later sections.

The group
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
of nonzero real numbers acts by multiplication on the set of all bases of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let a
projective basis
be an element of the corresponding quotient, namely a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
considered only up to scalar multiplication.

Let a tangent vector
V\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
be given, and represented by the tangent vector
\dot{\rho}=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}{}_{|t=0}
of a 1–parameter family of homomorphisms
\rho_{t}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
with
\rho_{0}=\rho
. Suppose that we are given the additional data, for each point
\widetilde{x}
of the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
, of a projective basis
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})
such that

(1)

the choice of
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})
is
\rho_{t}
–equivariant, in the sense that
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\gamma\widetilde{x})=\rho_{t}(\gamma)\left(\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})\right)
for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
and every point
\widetilde{x}
of
\widetilde{S}
;

(2)

for every
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{S}
, the projective basis
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})
depends differentiably on
t
.

Note that there is no requirement that the projective basis
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})
depends continuously on
\widetilde{x}
, so that it can just be constructed orbit by orbit for the action of
\pi_{1}(S)
on
\widetilde{S}
. In practice, we will only need these projective bases
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})
over finitely many orbits, corresponding to the preimage in
\widetilde{S}
of the vertex set of a triangulation of
S
.

By considering the coordinates of its elements, we can interpret the projective basis
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})
as a projective matrix
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})\in\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and we will use the same notation to represent the projective basis and the projective matrix. Namely,
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})\in\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the unique projective map sending the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to the projective basis
\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})
.
For every
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{S}
, we now consider the derivative

c^{0}(\widetilde{x})=\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})\mathcal{B}_{0}(\widetilde{x})^{-1}{}_{|t=0}\in\mathfrak{pgl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})=\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R}).

We can interpret
c^{0}
as an
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
–valued singular 0–cochain
c^{0}\in C^{0}\big(\widetilde{S};\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\big)
. We then consider its coboundary
c^{1}=dc^{0}\in C^{1}\big(\widetilde{S};\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})\big)
. Namely, if
\widetilde{k}
is a singular 1–simplex in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, considered as a path going from
\widetilde{x}_{-}
to
\widetilde{x}_{+}
, then

c^{1}(\widetilde{k})=c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{+})-c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{-}).

Lemma 9
.

The
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
–valued cochain
c^{1}
is closed and
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariant.

Proof.

The fact that
c^{1}
is closed is an immediate consequence of the fact that
c^{1}=dc^{0}
.

To prove the
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariance, let us first compute how
c^{0}
behaves under the action of
\pi_{1}(S)
. For
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{S}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
,

\displaystyle c^{0}(\gamma\widetilde{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{B}_{t}(\gamma\widetilde{x})\mathcal{B}_{0}(\gamma\widetilde{x})^{-1}{}_{|t=0}=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})\mathcal{B}_{0}(\widetilde{x})^{-1}\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\mathcal{B}_{0}(\widetilde{x})\mathcal{B}_{0}(\widetilde{x})^{-1}\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}+\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{0}(\gamma)\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})\mathcal{B}_{0}(\widetilde{x})^{-1}\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}+\rho_{0}(\gamma)\left(\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{B}_{t}(\widetilde{x})\mathcal{B}_{0}(\widetilde{x})^{-1}{}_{|t=0}\right)\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}+\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\gamma)\big(c^{0}(\widetilde{x})\big).

Therefore, if the 1–simplex
\widetilde{k}
goes from
\widetilde{x}_{-}
to
\widetilde{x}_{+}
,

\displaystyle c^{1}(\gamma\widetilde{k})

\displaystyle=c^{0}(\gamma\widetilde{x}_{+})-c^{0}(\gamma\widetilde{x}_{-})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\gamma)\big(c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{+})\big)-\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\gamma)\big(c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{-})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\gamma)\big(c^{1}(\widetilde{k})\big).

This proves that
c^{1}
is
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariant. Note that this is in general not true for
c^{0}
.
∎

As a consequence,
c^{1}
is a cocycle in the twisted chain complex
C^{\bullet}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
by definition of this chain complex, and defines a singular cohomology class
[c^{1}]\in H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
.

Lemma 10
.

The singular cohomology class
[c^{1}]\in H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
is equal to the Weil class
[c_{V}]\in H^{1}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)=H^{1}\left(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
associated to the same tangent vector
V\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
.

Proof.

To compare
[c^{1}]
to the Weil class
[c_{V}]
, we need to be more explicit with the correspondence between the group cohomology space
H^{1}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
and the singular cohomology space
H^{1}\left(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
.

The group cohomology of
\pi_{1}(S)
is defined as the simplicial cohomology of a complex
K
whose
n
–dimensional simplices
\sigma_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}}
are indexed by the elements
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n})
of the power
\pi_{1}(S)^{n}
, and where the boundary operator is defined by

\partial\sigma_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}}=\sigma_{\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3},\dots,\gamma_{n}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}\sigma_{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{i-1},\gamma_{i}\gamma_{i+1},\gamma_{i+2},\dots,\gamma_{n}}+(-1)^{n}\sigma_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n-1}}.

See for instance
[
Bro82
, II.3]
.

The twisted cohomology
H^{*}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
is defined as the homology of the chain complex of
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariant cochains on the universal cover
\widetilde{K}
of
K
. More precisely, the universal cover
\widetilde{K}
comes with a base vertex
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
in the preimage of the unique 0–simplex
\sigma_{0}
of
K
, and the 0–skeleton of
\widetilde{K}
is the orbit of
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
under the action of
\pi_{1}(K)=\pi_{1}(S)
. Then, the
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariant 1–cochain
c_{V}\in C^{1}(\widetilde{K};\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R}))
representing the Weil class
[c_{V}]\in H^{1}\left(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\right)
is uniquely determined by the property that, if
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma}
is the lift of
\sigma_{\gamma}
starting at the base point
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
,

c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R}).

Namely,
c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})
is what we earlier called
c_{V}(\gamma)
in the group cocycle interpretation of
c_{V}
. For a general
1
–simplex
\widetilde{\sigma}
of
\widetilde{K}
going from the 0–simplex
\alpha_{-}\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
to
\alpha_{+}\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}
, with
\alpha_{-}
,
\alpha_{+}\in\pi_{1}(S)
, the
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariance of
c_{V}
implies that

c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma})=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\alpha_{-})\big(c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\alpha_{-}^{-1}\alpha_{+}})\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\alpha_{-})\Big(\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\alpha_{-}^{-1}\alpha_{+})\rho_{0}(\alpha_{-}^{-1}\alpha_{+})^{-1}{}_{|t=0}\Big).

As a topological space,
K
is homotopy equivalent to
S
. Lift an arbitrary homotopy equivalence
f\colon K\to S
to a
\pi_{1}(S)
–equivariant map
\widetilde{f}\colon\widetilde{K}\to\widetilde{S}
, sending the base vertex
\widetilde{\sigma}_{0}\in\widetilde{K}
to a base point
\widetilde{x}_{0}=\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{0})\in\widetilde{S}
. The map
\widetilde{f}
then induces an isomorphism
H^{1}(f)\colon H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})\to H^{1}(K;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
.

To determine the image
H^{1}(f)\big([c^{1}]\big)\in H^{1}(K;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
of
[c^{1}]\in H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
, we first evaluate
c^{1}
on the 1–simplex
\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma}
associated to
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
.

\displaystyle c^{1}\big(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})\big)

\displaystyle=c^{0}\big(\widetilde{f}(\gamma\widetilde{\sigma}_{0})\big)-c^{0}\big(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{0})\big)=c^{0}(\gamma\widetilde{x}_{0})-c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\rho_{t}(\gamma)\rho_{0}(\gamma)^{-1}{}_{|t=0}+\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\gamma)\big(c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{0})\big)-c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})+\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\gamma)\big(c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{0})\big)-c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{0}),

using our earlier computation of
c^{0}(\gamma\widetilde{x}_{0})
in the proof of Lemma
9
. If we introduce an
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariant 0–cocycle
c^{0\,\prime}\in C^{0}(\widetilde{S};\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
defined by the property that

c^{0\,\prime}(\widetilde{x})=\begin{cases}\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho(\alpha)\big(c^{0}(\widetilde{x}_{0})\big)&\text{if }\widetilde{x}=\alpha\widetilde{x}_{0}\text{ for some }\alpha\in\pi_{1}(S)\\
0&\text{if }\widetilde{x}\text{ is not in the orbit of }\widetilde{x}_{0},\end{cases}

this can be rewritten as

\displaystyle c^{1}\big(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})\big)

\displaystyle=c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})+c^{0\,\prime}(\gamma\widetilde{x}_{0})-c^{0\,\prime}(\widetilde{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})+dc^{0\,\prime}\big(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{\gamma})\big).

By
\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho
–equivariance, this implies that

c^{1}\big(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\sigma})\big)-dc^{0\,\prime}\big(\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{\sigma})\big)=c_{V}(\widetilde{\sigma})

for every
1
–simplex
\widetilde{\sigma}
of
\widetilde{K}
. As a consequence, the isomorphism
H^{1}(f)\colon H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})\to H^{1}(K;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
sends
[c^{1}]\in H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
to

H^{1}(f)\big([c^{1}]\big)=H^{1}(f)\big([c^{1}-dc^{0\prime}]\big)=[c_{V}]

in
H^{1}(K;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})=H^{1}\big(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\big)
.
∎

The cocycle
c^{1}\in H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
developed in this section will enable us to localize the computation of the Weil class
[c_{V}]\in H^{1}\big(\pi_{1}(S);\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho}\big)
associated to a tangent vector
V\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
, in order to compute the cup product
c_{V_{1}}{\smallsmile}\,c_{V_{2}}
corresponding to two tangent vectors. For this purpose, we will not need to determine the full singular cocycle
c^{1}
, but just its evaluation on the 1–simplices of a suitably chosen triangulation of the surface
S
.

3.
Eruptions and shears

3.1.
Flags, triple ratios and double ratios

Recall that a (complete)
flag

F
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a nested family of linear subspaces
0=F^{(0)}\subset F^{(1)}\subset F^{(2)}\subset\dots\subset F^{(n-1)}\subset F^{(n)}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that each
F^{(a)}
has dimension
a
. We denote the space of flags in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The standard action of the linear group
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
induces an action of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which descends to an action of the projective group
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
on
\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The key ingredients for the results of
[
FG06
]
are certain invariants for the action of
\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
on sufficiently generic finite families of flags.

A flag triple
(E,F,G)\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{3}
satisfies the
Maximum Span Property
if

\dim(E^{(a)}+F^{(b)}+G^{(c)})=\min\{a+b+c,n\}

for every
a
,
b
,
c\in\{0,1,2,\dots,n\}
. In other words, this means that the span
E^{(a)}+F^{(b)}+G^{(c)}
of the three subspaces
E^{(a)}
,
F^{(b)}
,
G^{(c)}
is as large as possible. When this property is satisfied, we will also say that
(E,F,G)
is a
maximum-span flag triple
. Elementary considerations show that this is equivalent to the property that

\mathbb{R}^{n}=E^{(a)}\oplus F^{(b)}\oplus G^{(c)}

for every
a
,
b
,
c
with
a+b+c=n
.

This definition is exactly what is needed to define, for every
a
,
b
,
c\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=n
, the
(a,b,c)
–triple-ratio invariant
of a maximum-span flag triple
(E,F,G)\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{3}
, which is the quantity

X_{abc}(E,F,G)=\frac{e^{(a+1)}\wedge f^{(b)}\wedge g^{(c-1)}}{e^{(a-1)}\wedge f^{(b)}\wedge g^{(c+1)}}\frac{e^{(a)}\wedge f^{(b-1)}\wedge g^{(c+1)}}{e^{(a)}\wedge f^{(b+1)}\wedge g^{(c-1)}}\frac{e^{(a-1)}\wedge f^{(b+1)}\wedge g^{(c)}}{e^{(a+1)}\wedge f^{(b-1)}\wedge g^{(c)}}\in\mathbb{R}-\{0\}

where all
e^{(a^{\prime})}\in\mathsf{\Lambda}^{a^{\prime}}E^{(a^{\prime})}\cong\mathbb{R}
,
f^{(b^{\prime})}\in\mathsf{\Lambda}^{b^{\prime}}F^{(b^{\prime})}\cong\mathbb{R}
and
g^{(c^{\prime})}\in\mathsf{\Lambda}^{c^{\prime}}G^{(c^{\prime})}\cong\mathbb{R}
are arbitrary nonzero elements of the corresponding exterior powers, and where the quotients are taken for an arbitrary isomorphism
\mathsf{\Lambda}^{n}\mathbb{R}^{n}\cong\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 11

(
[
FG06
]
)
.

Given two maximum-span flag triples
(E,F,G)
and
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{3}
, there exists a projective map
\varphi\in\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
sending
(E,F,G)
to
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime})
if and only if

X_{abc}(E,F,G)=X_{abc}(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime})

for every
a
,
b
,
c\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=n
.

In addition, the projective map
\varphi
is unique when it exists.
∎

Similarly, let
(E,F;G,H)\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{4}
be a flag quadruple such that the flag triples
(E,F,G)
and
(E,F,H)
satisfy the Maximum Span Property. For
a
,
b\geqslant 1
with
a+b=n
, the
(a,b)
–double-ratio invariant
of
(E,F;G,H)
is defined as

X_{ab}(E,F;G,H)=-\frac{e^{(a)}\wedge f^{(n-a-1)}\wedge g^{(1)}}{e^{(a)}\wedge f^{(n-a-1)}\wedge h^{(1)}}\frac{e^{(a-1)}\wedge f^{(n-a)}\wedge h^{(1)}}{e^{(a-1)}\wedge f^{(n-a)}\wedge g^{(1)}}\in\mathbb{R}-\{0\}

where all
e^{(a^{\prime})}\in\mathsf{\Lambda}^{a^{\prime}}E^{(a^{\prime})}\cong\mathbb{R}
,
f^{(b^{\prime})}\in\mathsf{\Lambda}^{b^{\prime}}F^{(b^{\prime})}\cong\mathbb{R}
,
g^{(1)}\in\mathsf{\Lambda}^{1}G^{(1)}\cong\mathbb{R}
,
h^{(1)}\in\mathsf{\Lambda}^{1}H^{(1)}\cong\mathbb{R}
are arbitrary nonzero elements in the corresponding exterior powers.

Note that
X_{ab}(E,F;G,H)
does not depend on the whole flags
G
and
H
, but only on the lines
G^{(1)}
,
H^{(1)}
. In particular, the requirement that
(E,F,G)
and
(E,F,H)
satisfy the Maximum Span Property is an overkill if we only want
X_{ab}(E,F;G,H)
to be defined.

Proposition 12

(
[
FG06
]
)
.

Given two flag quadruples
(E,F;G,H)
and
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime};G^{\prime},H^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{4}
such that
(E,F,G)
,
(E,F,H)
,
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime})
and
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},H^{\prime})
satisfy the Maximum Span Property, there exists a projective map
\varphi\in\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
sending
(E,F;G,H)
to
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime};G^{\prime},H^{\prime})
if and only if

\displaystyle X_{abc}(E,F,G)

\displaystyle=X_{abc}(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle X_{abc}(E,F,H)

\displaystyle=X_{abc}(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},H^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\text{and }X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}(E,F;G,H)

\displaystyle=X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}(E^{\prime},F^{\prime};G^{\prime},H^{\prime})

for every
a
,
b
,
c
,
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=n
and
a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}=n
.

In addition, the projective map
\varphi
is unique when it exists.
∎

A basis
\mathcal{B}=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n}\}
for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
specifies two preferred flags: the
ascending flag

E
defined by the property that each subspace
E^{(a)}
is spanned by the first
a
vectors
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
, …,
e_{a}
of
\mathcal{B}
; and the
descending flag

F
for which each
F^{(b)}
is spanned by the last
b
vectors
e_{n-b+1}
,
e_{n-b+2}
, …,
e_{n}
.

We will frequently make use of the following elementary result, which was also the main ingredient in the proof of the uniqueness part of Propositions
11
and
12
.

Lemma 13
.

Let
(E,F,G)
,
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{3}
be two maximum-span flag triples. Then, there is a unique projective map
\varphi\in\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
sending the flag
E
to
E^{\prime}
, the flag
F
to
F^{\prime}
, and the line
G^{(1)}
to
G^{\prime(1)}
.

Proof.

An elementary argument provides a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n}\}
whose ascending flag is equal to
E
, whose descending flag is equal to
F
, and such that the line
G^{(1)}
is spanned by the sum
e_{1}+e_{2}+\dots+e_{n}
of the elements of
\mathcal{B}
. In addition, this basis is unique up to multiplication of the elements of
\mathcal{B}
by the same scalar. The result then follows by consideration of the basis
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
similarly associated to the flag triple
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime})
.
∎

3.2.
Connection with Fock-Goncharov invariants

Let
\widetilde{S}
be the universal cover of the surface
S
, and let
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
denote its circle at infinity. An important consequence of the Anosov property of Hitchin representations developed by Labourie
[
Lab06
]
is the following.

Proposition 14

(
[
Lab06
]
)
.

Let
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be a Hitchin representation. Then there exists a unique continuous map
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
that is
\rho
–equivariant, in the sense that

\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\gamma x)=\rho(\gamma)\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x)

for every
x\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
. ∎

The map
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the
flag map
of the Hitchin representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proposition 15

(
[
Lab06
,
FG06
]
)
.

The flag map
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of a Hitchin representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is
positive
, in the sense that:

(1)

For every distinct three points
x
,
y
,
z\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
, and every integers
a
,
b
,
c\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=n
, the triple-ratio

X_{abc}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(z)\big)

is well-defined and positive.

(2)

For every four points
x
,
y
,
u
,
v\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
such that
u
and
v
are in different components of
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}-\{x,y\}
, and for every integers
a
,
b\geqslant 1
with
a+b=n
, the double-ratio
X_{ab}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y);\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(u),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(v)\big)
is positive.
∎

We can now hint at the way the generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants
\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(U,s)
and
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
of a Hitchin representation are defined. These are based on the triple-ratio and double-ratio invariants of §
3.1
.

Let
\Psi
be a train track carrying the geodesic lamination
\lambda
, associated to a train track neighborhood
\Phi
of
\lambda
. Let
U
be a component of
S-\Phi
, and let
s
be one of the three corners of the closure of
U
. By the correspondence of Proposition
6
, the corner
s
determines a vertex
x
of the component
T
of
S-\lambda
that contains the component of
S-\Phi
corresponding to
U
. For the preimage
\widetilde{\lambda}
of
\lambda
in the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
, lift
T
to a component
\widetilde{T}
of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
and let
\widetilde{x}\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
be the vertex of
\widetilde{T}
corresponding to
x
. Index the other vertices of
\widetilde{T}
as
\widetilde{y}
and
\widetilde{z}
in such a way that
\widetilde{x}
,
\widetilde{y}
,
\widetilde{z}
occur in this order counterclockwise around
\widetilde{T}
, and consider the flags
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{x})
,
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{y})
,
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{z})\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
associated to these vertices by the flag map
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}
. Then, the invariant
\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(U,s)
is defined as

\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(U,s)=\log X_{abc}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{x}),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{y}),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{z})\big).

Note that the positivity of
X_{abc}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{x}),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{y}),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(\widetilde{z})\big)
guaranteed by Proposition
15
is needed for the logarithm to be defined.

The shearing invariant
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
is easily described in the very special case when there is a unique leaf
g
of
\lambda
that passes through the branch of
\Phi
corresponding to
e
. The orientation of the branch
e
determines an orientation for
g
, and
g
separates two components
T
and
T^{\prime}
of
S-\lambda
with
T
on the right of
g
and
T^{\prime}
on its left. Lift
g
to an oriented leaf
\widetilde{g}
of
\widetilde{\lambda}
, and let
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
be the two components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
that are adjacent to
\widetilde{g}
and respectively lift
T
and
T^{\prime}
. Let
x\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
be the positive endpoint of
\widetilde{g}
, let
y
be its negative endpoint, let
u
be the third vertex of
\widetilde{T}
, and let
v
be the third vertex of
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
. Then,

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)=\log X_{ab}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y);\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(u),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(v)\big)

in this special case. Note that the positivity of
X_{ab}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y);\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(u),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(v)\big)
is again critical.

This definition of
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
in this special case, as well as the definition of the triangle invariants
\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(U,s)
, are exactly those of the (non-generalized) Fock-Goncharov invariants of
[
FG06
]
. The definition of the shearing invariant
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
is much more elaborate in the generic case where there are infinitely (and possibly uncountably) many leaves of
\lambda
passing through the branch of
\Phi
corresponding to
e
, and requires the use of the shearing map discussed in §
4.2
; in particular, see Fact
22
.

3.3.
Left and right eruption maps

The notion of eruption, as developed in
[
SWZ20
]
, provides a very convenient tool to modify a map
\mathcal{F}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in such a way that one triple-ratio invariant
X_{abc}\big(\mathcal{F}(x),\mathcal{F}(y),\mathcal{F}(z)\big)
can be arbitrarily adjusted, while many other invariants
X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}\big(\mathcal{F}(x^{\prime}),\mathcal{F}(y^{\prime}),\mathcal{F}(z^{\prime})\big)
and
X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}\big(\mathcal{F}(x^{\prime}),\mathcal{F}(y^{\prime});\mathcal{F}(u^{\prime}),\mathcal{F}(v^{\prime})\big)
remain unchanged.

We will only need a very weak version of these eruptions. Given a maximum-span flag triple
(E,F,G)\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{3}
and integers
a
,
b
,
c\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=n
, we have a direct sum decomposition

\mathbb{R}^{n}=E^{(a)}\oplus F^{(b)}\oplus G^{(c)}.

The
left and right
(a,b,c)
–eruptions of amplitude
t\in\mathbb{R}
along
(E,F,G)
are the two projective maps
L^{abc}_{EFG}(t)
and
R^{abc}_{EFG}(t)\in\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
respectively defined as:

\displaystyle L^{abc}_{EFG}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathrm{Id}_{E^{(a)}}\oplus\mathrm{Id}_{F^{(b)}}\oplus\mathrm{Id}_{G^{(c)}}

\displaystyle R^{abc}_{EFG}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{E^{(a)}}\oplus\mathrm{e}^{t}\mathrm{Id}_{F^{(b)}}\oplus\mathrm{Id}_{G^{(c)}}.

We list the more important properties of these eruption maps.

Lemma 16
.

For every maximum-span flag triple
(E,F,G)\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{3}
, for every integers
a
,
b
,
c\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=n
, and for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(1)

L_{EFG}^{abc}(t)(E)=E
,
R_{EFG}^{abc}(t)(F)=F
;

(2)

L_{EFG}^{abc}(t)(G)=R_{EFG}^{abc}(t)(G)
;

(3)

L_{EFG}^{abc}(t)
and
R_{EFG}^{abc}(t)
respect the lines
E^{(1)}
,
F^{(1)}
and
G^{(1)}
;

(4)

for every integers
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
,
c^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=n
,

X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}\big(E,F,L_{EFG}^{abc}(t)(G)\big)=\begin{cases}X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}(E,F,G)&\text{if }(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})\neq(a,b,c)\\
\mathrm{e}^{t}X_{abc}(E,F,G)&\text{if }(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})=(a,b,c)\end{cases}

(5)

for every integers
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
,
c^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=n
and for every
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
L_{EFG}^{abc}(t)
commutes with
L_{EFG}^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})
;

(6)

for every integers
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}
,
c^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=n
and for every
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
R_{EFG}^{abc}(t)
commutes with
R_{EFG}^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Properties (1) and (2) are the content of
[
SWZ20
, Lem. 3.1]
, after composition of the two sides of each of these equalities with the map
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{t}{3}}\mathrm{Id}_{E^{(a)}}\oplus\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{3}}\mathrm{Id}_{F^{(b)}}\oplus\mathrm{Id}_{G^{(c)}}
.

Property (3) is obvious from definitions.

Property (4) is proved in
[
SWZ20
, Prop. 3.5(1)]
.

Properties (5–6) are the content of
[
SWZ20
, Prop. 3.14(1)]
.
∎

To explain our “left” and “right” terminology for eruptions, we can say that we will use them in situations where the flags
E
,
F
,
G\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are associated to points
x
,
y
,
z\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
where
x
sits to the left of
y
as seen from a base point, while
z
sits behind the geodesic
xy
as seen from the same base point. Then
L_{EFG}^{abc}(t)
and
R_{EFG}^{abc}(t)
will be applied to flags associated to points
w\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
that sit behind
xy
and respectively are to the left and right of
z
. See also the more general version of eruptions in
[
SWZ20
]
.

3.4.
Shearing maps

Shearing maps similarly modify the double-ratio invariants
X_{ab}(E,F;G,H)
, but are much simpler than eruptions.

Consider a flag pair
(E,F)\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{2}
that is
transverse
, in the sense that every linear subspace
E^{(a)}
is transverse to every
F^{(b)}
. Given
a
,
b\geqslant 1
with
a+b=n
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the
(a,b)
–shearing map of amplitude
t
along
(E,F)
is the projective map
S^{ab}_{EF}(t)\in\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
defined as

S^{ab}_{EF}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{t}\mathrm{Id}_{E^{(a)}}\oplus\mathrm{Id}_{F^{(b)}}.

Lemma 17
.

For every transverse flag pair
(E,F)\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{2}
, for every integers
a
,
b\geqslant 1
with
a+b=n
, and for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(1)

S_{EF}^{ab}(t)
respects the flags
E
and
F
;

(2)

for every
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}=n
, and for every
G
,
H\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that the flag triples
(E,F,G)
and
(E,F,H)
satisfy the Maximum Span Property,

X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}\big(E,F;G,S_{EF}^{ab}(t)(H)\big)=\begin{cases}X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}\big(E,F;G,H\big)&\text{if }(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\neq(a,b)\\
\mathrm{e}^{t}X_{ab}\big(E,F;G,H\big)&\text{if }(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=(a,b)\end{cases}

(3)

for every
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}=n
and every
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
S_{EF}^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})
commutes with
S_{EF}^{ab}(t)
.

Proof.

By transversality, there is a basis
\mathcal{B}
for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in which
E
is the ascending flag and
F
is the descending flag. Using this basis for computations easily provides a proof of the above properties.
∎

4.
The flag map of a variation of a Hitchin representation

We consider a variation of a Hitchin character
[\rho]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
, namely a nearby character
[\widehat{\rho}]\in\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
associated to small variations
\Delta\tau^{abc}
and
\Delta\sigma^{ab}
of the generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants of
[\rho]
, in the sense that

\displaystyle\tau^{abc}_{\widehat{\rho}}(U,s)

\displaystyle=\tau^{abc}_{\rho}(U,s)+\Delta\tau^{abc}(U,s)

\displaystyle\sigma^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\rho}}(e)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}_{\rho}(e)+\Delta\sigma^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}(e)

for every component
U
of the complement
S-\Psi
of a train track
\Psi
carrying the geodesic lamination
\lambda
, for every corner
s
of the triangle
U
, for every oriented branch
e
of
\Psi
, and for every
a
,
b
,
c
,
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}=n
.

This section is devoted to an explicit comparison between the flag maps
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\widehat{\rho}}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, in terms of these variations
\Delta\tau^{abc}
and
\Delta\sigma^{ab}
. This will lead us to a practical implementation of the scheme of §
2.2
to compute the Weil class
[c_{V}]\in H^{1}(S;\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\rho})
associated to the tangent vector
V\in T_{[\rho]}\mathrm{Hit}_{n}(S)
, in terms of the corresponding infinitesimal variations
\dot{\tau}^{abc}
and
\dot{\sigma}^{ab}
of the generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants of
[\rho]
.

4.1.
Lifting invariants from the train track
\Psi
to the geodesic lamination
\widetilde{\lambda}

To compare the flag maps
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\widehat{\rho}}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we need to lift our data to the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
. By construction, the train track
\Psi
is obtained by collapsing the ties of a train track neighborhood
\Phi
of
\lambda
.
Let
\widetilde{\lambda}\subset\widetilde{S}
be the preimage of the geodesic lamination
\lambda
, and let
\widetilde{\Phi}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
be the respective preimages of
\Phi
and
\Psi
.

The one-to-one correspondence of Proposition
6
provides us with the following rephrasing of the triangle invariants.

Lemma 18
.

The triangle invariant map

\tau_{\rho}^{abc}\colon\left\{(U,s);\ U\text{ component of }S-\Psi\text{ and }s\text{ corner of }U\right\}\to\mathbb{R}

uniquely determines a map

\tau_{\rho}^{abc}\colon\left\{(\widetilde{T},x);\ \widetilde{T}\text{ component of }\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}\text{ and }x\text{ vertex of }\widetilde{T}\right\}\to\mathbb{R},

denoted by the same symbol
\tau_{\rho}^{abc}
, such that

\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(\widetilde{T},x)=\tau_{\rho}^{abc}(U,s)

whenever the one-to-one correspondences of Proposition
6
associate
U
to the projection
T
of
\widetilde{T}
in
S
, and the corner
s
of
U
to the vertex of
T
that is the image of
x
under the projection.
∎

The interpretation of shear invariants in terms of components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
is more elaborate, but will play a critical role in our construction.

Lemma 19
.

The shear invariant map

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}\colon\left\{\text{oriented branches of }\Psi\right\}\to\mathbb{R}

uniquely determines a
\pi_{1}(S)
–equivariant map

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}\colon\left\{(\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime});\ \widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime}\text{ distinct components of }\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}\right\}\to\mathbb{R},

denoted by the same symbol
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}
, such that the following two conditions are satisfied.

(1)

For every oriented branch
e
of the train track
\Psi
and for every lift of
e
to a branch
\widetilde{e}
of the preimage
\widetilde{\Psi}
of
\Psi
in
\widetilde{S}
, let
\widetilde{U}
and
\widetilde{U}^{\prime}
be the two components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\Psi}
that are adjacent to
\widetilde{e}
, indexed so that
\widetilde{U}
sits to the left of
\widetilde{e}
for the orientation of
\widetilde{e}
. Then, if
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
are the two components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
that contain the components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\Phi}
respectively associated to
\widetilde{U}
and
\widetilde{U}^{\prime}
,

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime})=\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e).

(2)

Let
\widetilde{T}
,
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
,
\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime}
be three components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
such that
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
separates
\widetilde{T}
from
\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime}
, in the sense that
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime}
sit in different components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
. In particular, there exist exactly two sides of the triangle
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
that separate
\widetilde{T}
from
\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime}
; let
x
be the vertex of
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
that is common to these two sides. Then:

(a)

if
x
sits to the left of
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
as seen from
\widetilde{T}

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime})=\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime})+\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\widetilde{T}^{\prime},\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime})-\sum_{b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=n-a}\tau_{\rho}^{ab^{\prime}c^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{\prime},x);

(b)

if
x
sits to the right of
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
as seen from
\widetilde{T}

\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime})=\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime})+\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(\widetilde{T}^{\prime},\widetilde{T}^{\prime\prime})-\sum_{a^{\prime}+c^{\prime}=n-b}\tau_{\rho}^{bc^{\prime}a^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{\prime},x).

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the alternative interpretation of relative tangent cycles developed in
[
BD17
, §4.7]
.
∎

Actually, the invariants
\tau_{\rho}^{abc}
and
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}
were originally defined in
[
BD17
]
in the form of Lemmas
18
and
19
, before being expressed in terms of data associated to a train track carrying the geodesic lamination
\lambda
.

We will mostly rely on the natural extension of Lemmas
18
and
19
to the variations
\Delta\tau^{abc}
and
\Delta\sigma^{ab}
of the generalized Fock-Goncharov invariants.

4.2.
Slithering maps

The slithering maps are fundamental objects in the definition of the shearing invariants
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}(e)
in
[
BD17
]
.

Consider two oriented leaves
xy
and
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
of the geodesic lamination
\widetilde{\lambda}
in the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
, oriented in parallel in the sense that their endpoints
x
,
y
,
y^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime}\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
occur in this order (clockwise or counterclockwise) around the circle at infinity
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
. The flag map
\mathcal{F}_{\rho}\colon\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}\to\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of a Hitchin representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
associates flags
E=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x)
,
F=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y)
,
E^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x^{\prime})
,
F^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Flag}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to these endpoints. By, for instance, Lemma
13
applied to
(E,F,G)
and
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},G^{\prime})
with
G=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(z)
and
G^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(z^{\prime})
associated to additional points
z
,
z^{\prime}\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
, there exists a linear isomorphism
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
sending
E^{\prime}
to
E
and
F^{\prime}
to
F
. In fact, there exist many such linear isomorphisms, since the stabilizer of the pair
(E,F)
in
\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
has dimension
n
. The slithering construction uses the maximality of the geodesic lamination
\widetilde{\lambda}
to single out a preferred isomorphism
\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
sending
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime})
to
(E,F)
.

More precisely, first consider the case where the two leaves
xy
and
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
have a common endpoint
x=x^{\prime}
; in particular,
E=E^{\prime}
. Then
\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the unique linear map that sends
(E^{\prime},F^{\prime})
to
(E,F)
and is
unipotent
in the sense that all its eigenvalues are equal to 1. The unipotent property is equivalent to saying that, if we choose an arbitrary basis
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
, …,
e_{n}
for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that each
E^{(a)}
is spanned by
\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{a}\}
and each
F^{(b)}
is spanned by
\{e_{n-b+1},e_{n-b+2},\dots,e_{n}\}
, the matrix of
\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}
in this basis is upper triangular with all diagonal terms equal to 1.

The slithering map
\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}
is similarly defined when
y=y^{\prime}
.

In the general case, let
\widetilde{T}_{1}
,
\widetilde{T}_{2}
, …,
\widetilde{T}_{i_{0}}
be a family of components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
separating the leaf
xy
from
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
, indexed in this order from
xy
to
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
in the sense that each
\widetilde{T}_{i}
separates
xy
from
\widetilde{T}_{i+1}
. Let
x_{i}y_{i}
be the side of
\widetilde{T}_{i}
that faces
xy
, and let
x_{i}^{\prime}y_{i}^{\prime}
be the side that faces
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
, with the parallelisms between
xy
,
x_{i}y_{i}
,
x_{i}^{\prime}y_{i}^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
compatible with orientations. Note that, for each
i
, either
x_{i}=x_{i}^{\prime}
or
y_{i}=y_{i}^{\prime}
, so that the slithering map
\Sigma_{x_{i}y_{i},x_{i}^{\prime}y_{i}^{\prime}}
is defined by the above special cases. Then, the
slithering map

\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined as the limit of

\Sigma_{x_{1}y_{1},x_{1}^{\prime}y_{1}^{\prime}}\circ\Sigma_{x_{2}y_{2},x_{2}^{\prime}y_{2}^{\prime}}\circ\dots\circ\Sigma_{x_{i_{0}}y_{i_{0}},x_{i_{0}}^{\prime}y_{i_{0}}^{\prime}}

as the family
\{\widetilde{T}_{1},\widetilde{T}_{2},\dots,\widetilde{T}_{i_{0}}\}
tends to the set of all components of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
separating
xy
from
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
. See
[
BD17
, §5.1]
for a proof that the limit exists, and for a proof of the following fact.

Lemma 20
.

The slithering map
\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}
sends the flag
E^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x^{\prime})
to
E=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x)
, and the flag
F^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y^{\prime})
to
F=\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y)
.
∎

We note the following elementary properties.

Lemma 21
.

Let
xy
,
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime\prime}
be three oriented leaves of the geodesic lamination
\widetilde{\lambda}
that are parallel to each other. Then

\displaystyle\Sigma_{yx,y^{\prime}x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}\circ\Sigma_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\Sigma_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime},xy}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}^{-1}.\qed

The slithering maps play a critical role in the definition of the shearing invariants
\sigma_{\rho}^{ab}
. Let
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
be two components of the complement
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
. Index the vertices of
\widetilde{T}
as
x
,
y
,
z\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}

clockwise
in this order around
\widetilde{T}
, and in such a way that
xy
is the side facing
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
. Then index the vertices of
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
as
x^{\prime}
,
y^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime}\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
this time
counterclockwise
in this order, and so that
x^{\prime}y^{\prime}
is the side facing
\widetilde{T}
. Using the correspondence of Lemma
19
, the shearing invariant is defined in
[
BD17
, §5.2]
as follows.

Fact 22
.

With the vertex labelling conventions above,

\displaystyle\sigma^{ab}_{\rho}(\widetilde{T},\widetilde{T}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\log X_{ab}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y);\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(z),\Sigma_{xy,x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(z^{\prime}))\big)

\displaystyle=\log X_{ab}\big(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(x^{\prime}),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(y^{\prime});\Sigma_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime},xy}(\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(z)),\mathcal{F}_{\rho}(z^{\prime})\big),

where the double-ratio
X_{ab}(E,F;G,H)
is defined in
§
3.1
.

4.3.
Variation of slithering maps

We now return to the situation considered at the beginning of this §
4
, with two Hitchin representations
\rho
,
\widehat{\rho}\colon\pi_{1}(S)\to\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\displaystyle\tau^{abc}_{\widehat{\rho}}(U,s)

\displaystyle=\tau^{abc}_{\rho}(U,s)+\Delta\tau^{abc}(U,s)

\displaystyle\sigma^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}_{\widehat{\rho}}(e)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}_{\rho}(e)+\Delta\sigma^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}(e)

for every component
U
of the complement
S-\Psi
of a train track
\Psi
carrying the geodesic lamination
\lambda
, for every corner
s
of the triangle
U
, for every oriented branch
e
of
\Psi
, and for every
a
,
b
,
c
,
a^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}\geqslant 1
with
a+b+c=a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}=n
.

Let
\widetilde{T}_{0}
be a component of
\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{\lambda}
, which we will use as a base component, and let
xy
be a leaf of the geodesic lamination
\widetilde{\lambda}
. We choose the indexing so that
x
is to the left of
y
in
\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
, as seen from
\widetilde{T}_{0}
. Index the vertices of
\widetilde{T}_{0}

clockwise
as
u_{0}
,
v_{0}
,
w_{0}\in\partial_{\infty}\widetilde{S}
so that the side
u_{0}v_{0}
faces
xy
. The clockwise convention is designed so that the orientations of
u_{0}v_{0}
is parallel to that of
xy
. See Figure
7
.

We want to compare the slithering maps
